The Conference also operated a Diversion Information Exchange Forum and expressed concern about low reporting compliance.", "question_ids": ["1B.6", "2B.5"], "polarity": "favorable", "period_fit": "The Arms Trade Treaty Conference and related work occurred in 2023, within the target period.", "ruler_attribution": "The source documents an official Mexican position in a multilateral forum, but does not show that López Obrador personally authored or implemented the position.", "limitations": ["No domestic Mexican arms-transfer, licensing, enforcement, or suspension decision is described.", "The passage does not establish the outcome or implementation of Mexico’s proposal beyond its submission and the Conference’s general response."], "start_unit": 103, "end_unit": 104, "locator": "page 103 through page 104", "exact_excerpt": "Arms Trade Treaty\nThe Arms Trade Treaty[1] was created to establish common standards for international trade in\nconventional arms and to eradicate the illicit trade in conventional weapons. At the end of\n2023, its total number of States parties stood at 113, with 29 signatory States that had not yet\nratified the Treaty.\n\n\nNinth Conference of States Parties\n\nThe ninth Conference of States Parties took place from 21 to 25 August, presided by Seong-\nmee Yoon (Republic of Korea). The Conference was attended by 89 States parties, 18\nsignatory States and four States not party to the Treaty. In addition, nine international and\nregional organizations and 43 civil society organizations participated as observers (for the list\nof participants, see ATT/CSP9/2023/SEC/772/Conf.PartList.Rev1). In preparation, participants\nheld two series of meetings of the Treaty’s three working groups,[2] and two informal\npreparatory meetings on 17 February and 12 May.\n\n\nThe High Representative for Disarmament Affairs delivered a video statement during the\nopening session of the Conference, underlining that the Treaty offered important\nopportunities to consider humanitarian and human rights perspectives. She welcomed the\npriority topic chosen by the presidency, “Role of industry in responsible transfers of\nconventional arms”, noting that by adopting robust procedures, industry entities can make the\ntransfer chain less vulnerable to diversion.\n\n\nThe Conference began with a thematic discussion on the priority theme chosen by the\npresidency (ATT/CSP9/2023/PRES/766/Conf.WP.Ind). Two of the panellists, the Republic of\nKorea and Romania, outlined their respective national administrative policies for engaging\nwith representatives of industry. One of the speakers presented the Guiding Principles on\nBusiness and Human Rights (A/HRC/17/31, annex), endorsed by the Human Rights Council in\nits resolution 17/4 of 2011, and stressed that the tool could be used to further industry’s role in\nupholding human rights. She further underlined that under the Guiding Principles,\nGovernments could use a wide variety of tools to ensure respect for human rights. In a\nworking paper submitted to the Conference, Austria, Ireland and Mexico stressed the\ncomplementarity between the Arms Trade Treaty and the Guiding Principles (ATT/\nCSP9/2023/AUT-IRL-MEX/774/Conf.WP). In its final report, the Conference welcomed the\nGuiding Principles, encouraging States parties to continue discussions on how the Guiding\nPrinciples, human rights and international humanitarian law instruments apply in the context\nof the Arms Trade Treaty (ATT/CSP9/2023/SEC/773/Conf.FinRep.Rev2).\n\n\nDuring the week, the Diversion Information Exchange Forum, established by the sixth\nConference of States Parties (ATT/CSP6/2020/SEC/635/Conf.FinRep.Rev1), met and\n\n\n                                                93\n\nprovided an opportunity for States parties and signatory States to exchange information on\nconcrete cases of detected or suspected diversion and to share concrete, operational,\ndiversion-related information as contemplated in articles 11 and 15 of the Treaty. The Chair of\nthe Exchange Forum, France, briefed the Conference and welcomed the presentations made\nby four States. The Conference encouraged all States parties and signatory States to present\nat forthcoming meetings of the Exchange Forum cases of diversion, which the President of\nthe tenth Conference of States Parties would be expected to organize in accordance with the\nbody’s terms of reference (ATT/CSP6.DIEF/2020/CHAIR/632/Conf.DIEFToRs).\n\n\nThe Conference emphasized the importance of transparency and reporting and expressed its\nconcerns about the low rate of compliance with reporting obligations. In keeping with a\ndecision from the seventh Conference, States parties have the option to ask for Arms Trade\nTreaty reports to be submitted also to the United Nations Register of Conventional Arms. The\naim is to reduce reporting fatigue and bolster reporting under the Register. In 2023, the Arms\nTrade Treaty Secretariat transmitted 31 reports to the Office for Disarmament Affairs through\nthis sharing agreement.\n\n\nIn addition, the Conference considered the reports of the Treaty’s working groups on effective\ntreaty implementation, treaty universalization, and transparency and reporting, as well as\nvarious documents submitted by the Management Committee.[3] However, due to financial\ndifficulties and at the recommendation of the Management Committee, the Conference\ndecided to hold fewer meetings in 2024 on a trial basis for one year. Specifically, instead of\nholding two in-person sessions for the working groups as they had done in previous years, the\nStates parties opted to hold just one session over four days. Likewise, they chose to convene\nonly one in-person preparatory meeting over two days for the tenth Conference of States\nParties, diverging from their past practice of having two such meetings.\n\n\nThe Conference decided to hold the tenth Conference of States Parties in Geneva from 19 to\n23 August. It elected Razvan Rusu (Romania) as President, and Australia, Japan, Namibia and\nParaguay as Vice-Presidents. Furthermore, the Conference decided that the informal\npreparatory meetings and the meetings of the working groups would also be held in Geneva.\nThe specific dates for those meetings remained undecided, to be confirmed by the President\nof the tenth Conference of States Parties at a later date.\n\n\nSmall arms and light weapons\n\nProgramme of Action on Small Arms and Light Weapons\n\nIn his policy brief A New Agenda for Peace, launched in July, the Secretary-General recognized\nthat small arms and light weapons and their ammunition were the leading cause of violent\ndeaths in conflict and non-conflict settings alike. The Meeting also received support from the Convention’s Technical\nSecretariat, jointly staffed by the OAS Department of Public Security and the OAS Department\nagainst Transnational Organized Crime.\n\n\n\n\n                                               161\n\nCountering firearms trafficking\n\nAt the fifty-fourth meeting of the Group of Experts for the Control of Money-Laundering, held\non 23 and 24 May, delegations approved a study on best practices for coordination by\nauthorities in confiscating materials in cases of money-laundering used to support illicit arms\ntrafficking. The Working Sub-Group on Forfeiture and International Cooperation developed\nthe study with technical support from the OAS Department against Transnational Organized\nCrime.\n\n\nMeanwhile, through its Department of Public Security, OAS continued to provide its member\nStates with comprehensive assistance in strengthening their capacities against illicit firearms\nand ammunition proliferation and trafficking. Its assistance benefited from European Union\nfunds and emphasized legislative support, through-life management of weapons and\nammunition, armed violence prevention, and regional coordination mechanisms. In 2023, the\nDepartment of Public Security organized the delivery of capacity-building technical and\nmaterial assistance for weapons and ammunition management to 19 countries in Latin\nAmerica and the Caribbean. That assistance included training 1,011 national personnel,\nimproving security conditions in five storage areas, installing inventory control software in five\nnational defence and security institutions, and destroying 37,518 firearms and 110 tons of\nammunition. Additionally, a partnership with Spain enabled 11 military and police officers from\nseven Latin American countries to be certified in Level 3 explosive ordnance disposal.\n\n\nTo support efforts to prevent armed violence, the Department of Public Security supported\n1,100 at-risk youths, in Tela (Honduras) and in Lima, who participated in daily orchestra classes\naimed at improving their life skills. It also launched two massive open online courses on\npreventing armed violence and inaugurated the Center for Psychosocial Support of Victims of\nArmed Violence, in Tela.\n\n\nThe Department of Public Security also collaborated with the United Nations Regional Centre\nfor Peace, Disarmament and Development in Latin America and the Caribbean to launch the\ndevelopment of a Central American road map to prevent the illicit proliferation and trafficking\nof firearms and ammunition. With funding from the European Union, the Department of Public\nSecurity organized several rounds of awareness-raising and dialogue sessions with national\nauthorities, concluding with an in-person workshop to gather inputs for the future road map.\nThe anticipated plan was scheduled for adoption in 2024, with all Central American countries,\nexcept Nicaragua, engaging in the initiative.\n\n\nHumanitarian demining\n\nThe Department of Public Security’s comprehensive mine action programme continued to\nsupport Colombia in addressing explosive ordnance contamination, in line with a 2012\n\n\n\n                                                162\n\nbilateral agreement. In 2023, it supported quality management for seven humanitarian\ndemining operators, comprising two State/military entities and five humanitarian non-\ngovernmental organizations. The Department conducted seven evaluations of operational\nprocedures and 5,167 field monitoring visits, and it verified the decontamination of 314,587\nsquare metres of land.\n\n\nIn Colombia, the Department’s mine action programme delivered explosive ordnance risk\neducation directly to the public. Throughout the year, 11,689 people in 16 municipalities across\neight departments participated in regular explosive ordnance risk reduction activities. With\nrespect to age and gender, the group comprised 1,842 women, 1,592 men, 4,079 girls and\n4,176 boys.[9] Of those participants, 3,151 people in high-risk areas (447 women, 335 men, 1,173\ngirls and 1,196 boys) benefited from a special educational methodology emphasizing rapid\nresponse.[10] Furthermore, 20 survivors of explosives received assistance in accessing state\nmedical services and additional opportunities for sustainable livelihoods.\n\n\nSupport for the implementation of Security Council resolution 1540 (2004)\n\nIn 2023, the secretariat of the Inter-American Committee against Terrorism maintained its role\nin implementing Security Council resolution 1540 (2004) in the Americas, including through\nefforts to counter biological and chemical threats.\n\n\nThe Committee support ed eight countries[11] in strengthening biosafety and biosecurity\nmeasures in line with the resolution, emphasizing three priorities: (a) helping to align national\nlegislation with international standards; (b) conducting capacity-building activities, including\nbiosafety and biosecurity training of 250 technicians, scientists and laboratory personnel; and\n(c) undertaking outreach and awareness-raising, including through virtual events, to highlight\nbioterrorism and other related threats and establish a regional network of biosafety and\nbiosecurity professionals. The Committee also completed biosecurity threat assessments for\nfour countries and the broader region,[12] published four reports on its website, and analysed\nchemical weapons threats in key free trade zones, presenting the findings in international law\nenforcement forums.\n\n\nAs hemispheric coordinator for resolution 1540 (2004) in Latin America and the Caribbean, the\nCommittee facilitated two peer review exercises: (a) an exercise conducted in Mexico City in\nSeptember by Brazil, Chile and Mexico with a focus on biological weapons non-proliferation;\nand (b) an exercise in November and December between Ecuador and Paraguay on\nimplementing the Biological Weapons Convention. Nuclear security focuses on the prevention and\ndetection of and response to criminal or intentional unauthorized acts involving or directed at\nnuclear material, other radioactive material, associated facilities or associated activities.\nResponsibility for nuclear security within a State rests entirely with that State, in accordance\nwith its respective national and international obligations.\n\n\nThe Agency continued to assist States upon request in their national efforts to establish and\nmaintain effective and comprehensive nuclear security of all nuclear and other radioactive\nmaterial at all times. The Council required all States to\nadopt and enforce appropriate laws to that effect, as well as other effective measures to\nprevent the proliferation of those weapons and their means of delivery to non-State actors, in\nparticular for terrorist purposes.\n\n\nIn 2023, the Committee established pursuant to resolution 1540 (2004) (1540 Committee) was\nchaired by Hernán Pérez Loose from 31 January to 28 November, by Andrés Montalvo from 29\nNovember to 14 December, and by José De La Gasca from 15 to 31 December. In accordance\nwith resolution 2663 (2022), by which the Council extended the 1540 Committee’s mandate for\n10 years, until 30 November 2032, the Committee adopted its twentieth programme of work,\ncovering the period from 1 February 2023 to 31 January 2024 (S/2023/117, annex). The\nprogramme of work organized the Committee’s work around four pillars: (a) monitoring and\nimplementation; (b) assistance; (c) cooperation with international, regional and subregional\norganizations; and (d) transparency and outreach. The Office for Disarmament Affairs, the\nDepartment of Political and Peacebuilding Affairs, and the 1540 Committee’s Group of Experts\ncontinued to support the Committee in implementing its programme of work.\n\n\nNational implementation\n\nBy paragraph 4 of resolution 1540 (2004), the Security Council mandated States to submit a\nfirst report on the steps they had taken or intend to take to implement the resolution. By the\nend of 2023, 185 of the 193 Member States had submitted their first report, while 8 States had\n\n\n                                                 209\n\nyet to do so.[8] Furthermore, 124 Member States had submitted at least one additional report.\nAs of the end of the year, Member States had submitted a total of 537 first and additional\nnational reports on steps taken to implement resolution 1540 (2004).\n\n\nThe Security Council encouraged States in resolution 1977 (2011) and its successors to provide\nadditional information on their implementation efforts, including, voluntarily, on their laws and\nregulations and on States’ effective practices. In 2023, India, Türkiye and Uruguay submitted\nadditional information, bringing the number of States that had done so to 115.\n\n\nTo facilitate dialogue with the 1540 Committee on implementing resolution 1540 (2004), States\nare encouraged to submit up-to-date information on national points of contact, preferably\nboth in their capitals and at their respective permanent missions in New York. In 2023,\nresponding to renewed calls from the 1540 Committee, 43 States either named new or\nupdated existing points of contact, bringing the total number of States with designated points\nof contact for resolution 1540 (2004) to 146.\n\n\nThe Security Council encouraged States, by resolution 1977 (2011) and subsequent\nresolutions, to prepare, on a voluntary basis, national implementation action plans to map out\ntheir priorities and plans for implementing key provisions of resolution 1540 (2004). In 2023,\nMadagascar submitted such a plan, bringing the total, as at 31 December, to 47 voluntary\nnational implementation action plans, which were adopted by 38 States.\n\n\nThe Office for Disarmament Affairs supported several additional Member States in initiating\nprocesses to adopt voluntary national implementation action plans. In 2023, the Office\norganized workshops in Phnom Penh from 25 to 27 April, in Yaoundé from 12 to 14 September,\nin Bangkok from 10 to 12 October, in Abuja from 16 to 18 October, and in Vientiane from 28 to\n30 November. Those workshops convened various national ministries and agencies\nresponsible for implementing resolution 1540 (2004). The aim was to raise their awareness\nabout the resolution’s objectives and obligations, review the status of national\nimplementation, identify areas needing improvement, and establish priorities and targets for\nthe action plans.\n\n\nAssistance\n\nIn its resolution 2663 (2022), the Security Council reiterated that many Member States still\nrequired assistance in implementing resolution 1540 (2004) and emphasized the importance\nof improving the 1540 Committee’s matchmaking mechanism to ensure effective, timely and\ntailored assistance. The 1540 Committee initiated a review of its current assistance\nmechanism, established in 2018, including by conducting periodic reviews of pending\nassistance requests. In 2023, Iraq, Kenya, Kyrgyzstan and Madagascar submitted new\n\n\n\n\n                                                210\n\nassistance requests to the Committee. Additionally, Mexico and Zambia updated their\npreviously submitted requests from 2019 and 2020, respectively.\n\n\nCooperation with international, regional and subregional organizations\n\nIn resolution 1540 (2004) and its successors,[9] the Security Council recognized the need to\nenhance the coordination of efforts at the national, subregional, regional and international\nlevels to strengthen a global response to the threat from non-State actors. Likewise, the\nCouncil recognized the role and efforts of international, regional and subregional\norganizations, including United Nations bodies, in supporting Member States in their\nimplementation of resolution 1540 (2004), and called for continued cooperation in that regard.\n\n\nAccordingly, the Office of Disarmament Affairs engaged States and regional organizations to\nfoster and facilitate cooperation, coordination and exchanges at the subregional level. On 7\nand 8 March, in cooperation with Japan, a stakeholders meeting in Tokyo brought together\nrepresentatives from 14 States in the Asia-Pacific region.[10] In Africa, the Office collaborated\nwith Kenya and the Intergovernmental Authority on Development to convene a subregional\nworkshop on 14 and 15 March in Nairobi, drawing representatives from 7 States in the region.\n[11]\n       Members of the 1540 Committee also attended the regional workshops, which were\nintended to enrich States’ understanding of their obligations under resolution 1540 (2004) and\nthe status of its implementation in the respective subregions. The workshop offered States a\nplatform to share their experiences in implementing the resolution and discuss opportunities\nfor cross-border cooperation. (least free) to 100 (most free).\nSee the methodology\nand\nreport acknowledgements.\nheader1\n\nOverview\nInternet connectivity continues to expand in Mexico, driven by steady improvements to the country’s telecommunications infrastructure. The online environment remains a robust space for political mobilization, social discourse, and journalistic investigations. However, several threats remain to internet freedom in the country. Mexico continues to be one of the world’s deadliest countries for journalists, and online journalists are regularly targeted with harassment, threats, and physical violence, contributing to a climate of self-censorship. During the coverage period, evidence emerged that state actors, with probable links to the military, have continued to conduct illegal surveillance of journalists and human rights defenders. Additionally, President Andrés Manuel López Obrador and his political allies have taken actions to undermine regulators, including those that oversee the telecommunications industry and enforce data protection standards, weaking the effectiveness and independence of these bodies.\nMexico has been an electoral democracy since 2000, and alternation in power between parties is routine at both the federal and state levels. However, the country suffers from severe rule of law deficits that limit full citizen enjoyment of political rights and civil liberties. Violence perpetrated by organized criminals, corruption among government officials, human rights abuses by both state and nonstate actors, and rampant impunity are among the most visible of Mexico’s many governance challenges.\nheader2\nKey Developments, June 1, 2022 - May 31, 2023\nGovernment failure to appointment commissioners for several regulatory bodies—including the Federal Telecommunications Institute (IFT), the Federal Economic Competition Commission (COFECE), and the National Institute for Transparency, Access to Information, and Personal Data Protection (INAI)—seriously weakened their ability to function as independent entities during the coverage period (see A5 and C6).\nAn April 2023 legislative proposal to create a Federal Cybersecurity Law, which remains pending in the Chamber of Deputies, contained several potentially problematic provisions for content regulation, the criminalization of online expression, and user privacy (see B3, C2, and C5).\nCoordinated progovernment networks continued to manipulate online discussion in favor of President López Obrador and attack his political opponents, including on platforms that receive public funding (see B5).\nA series of journalistic investigations uncovered evidence that several individuals, including journalists, human rights defenders, and the undersecretary of human rights, had been surveilled using Pegasus spyware. Despite denials from President López Obrador, reporting established probable links that the Mexican military was responsible for this illegal surveillance (see C5).\nThe threat of violence continued to seriously impede the ability of journalists to report online. During the coverage period, at least three journalists were killed in potential retaliation for their online reporting, though a motive has not been confirmed in any of the cases (see C7).\nA\nObstacles to Access\nA1\n1.00-6.00 pts\n0-6 pts\nDo infrastructural limitations restrict access to the internet or the speed and quality of internet connections?\n5.00\n5\n6.00\n6\nInternet connectivity and service quality continue to improve in Mexico.\n1\nAccording to the latest data from the International Telecommunication Union (ITU), Mexico’s internet penetration rate stood at 76 percent in 2021.\n2\nThe National Institute of Statistics and Geography (INEGI) and IFT reported that there were 93.1 million internet users in the country as of 2022.", "excerpt_sha256": "ffad153f90532002be7ebb0e614f78847ff188764cb9a3895c5e8221ff489402", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0026-E015", "source_id": "SRC-26432e3d86aa471d", "url": "https://freedomhouse.org/country/mexico/freedom-net/2023", "title": "Mexico: Freedom on the Net 2023 Country Report", "publisher": "Freedom House", "raw_sha256": "55052483d1e8d22f7617e18dfe2b38b2b0aba2027783df80646eae8053c9bdea", "fact_summary": "Freedom House reported that Mexico had no documented nationwide internet blocking and that courts invalidated disproportionate website blocking and the biometric cell-phone registry, but also described government underreporting of content-removal requests, pressure on journalists, a 2023 cybersecurity bill requiring rapid takedowns and broad criminal penalties, and proposals risking censorship and militarized cybersecurity; Social networking sites and international blog-hosting services are available in Mexico.\nB2\n1.00-4.00 pts\n0-4 pts\nDo state or nonstate actors employ legal, administrative, or other means to force publishers, content hosts, or digital platforms to delete content, particularly material that is protected by international human rights standards?\n2.00\n2\n4.00\n4\nState and nonstate actors have increasingly used legal threats and other methods to pressure social media platforms, web-hosting providers, and individual users to remove content in recent years. The full scope and nature of government requests to remove content remains unknown, as the government underreports its requests. In a 2021 report, Article 19 estimated that only 14 percent of government removal requests reported by social media platforms on their transparency reports have been declared by various government offices through freedom of information (FOIA) requests.\n1\nAttempts to remove content frequently target journalistic information posted online. Article 19 recorded 12 removals of journalistic content in 2022.\n2\nIn April 2023, Supreme Court Judge Yasmín Esquivel Mossa filed a complaint against journalist Lourdes Mendoza, who had tweeted photos of Mossa vacationing in Canada accompanied by critical comments about the judge. Mossa asked a court to order the removal of the photos and the deletion of Mendoza’s Twitter account, on the grounds that her minor son appears in one of them and the comments allegedly “incite hatred.” The court ultimately ordered Mendoza and other journalists to remove or blur the photos to protect the identity of Mossa’s son, but did not force Mendoza to delete her account.\n3\n\nIn April 2022, during the previous coverage period, a Yucatán judge ordered six online outlets to remove their reporting on alleged corruption by a former government official and the related revocation of her pension. The order gave the outlets 24 hours to comply, and threatened sanctions outlined in Article 104 of the criminal code, which allows for fines and arrests of up to 36 hours, for noncompliance.\n4\nPowerful actors have successfully had content removed by claiming it violates privacy regulations and the Federal Copyright Law (see B3). In February 2022, Rest of World reported on the activities of Spanish reputation management company Eliminalia in Mexico, where the firm launched in 2015. Eliminalia has been found to use false copyright claims and legal notices to get online content taken down on behalf of its powerful clients, including businesspeople and politically connected individuals in Mexico. Of a list of 17,000 URLs that were reportedly targeted for removal on clients’ behalf between 2015 and 2019, more than 2,000 were identified for removal by clients based in Mexico.\n5\nIn one case, Eliminalia reportedly used dubious legal notices to force Mexican journalist Daniel Sánchez to remove a 2018 story he published in the outlet Página 66, which had detailed past links to corruption of a Mexican video surveillance firm, Interconecta, that the Campeche state governor had contracted with. Sánchez was obliged to remove the article after Eliminalia seemingly created a falsified article to launch a US-based copyright claim against him in January 2020.\n6\nSánchez’s investigation for Página 66 was reportedly one of 13 URLs that Grupo Altavista, the parent company of Interconecta, hired Eliminalia to remove in April 2019.\n7\nIn another case reported by the Organized Crime and Corruption Reporting Project (OCCRP) in February 2023, former political candidate Miguel Ángel Colorado Cessa allegedly paid €9,000 to Eliminalia to remove news stories that linked him and his brother to the Mexican cartel Los Zetas.\n8\nFacebook restricted 17,847 pieces of content between January and June 2022, including 46 items related to violations of electoral law and 17,801 items in response to Federal Commission for the Protection against Sanitary Risks (COFEPRIS) reports of unsafe products.\n9\nDuring that period, Mexico had the second highest number of Facebook content restrictions in the world, after South Korea.\n10\nBetween July and December 2022, Google reported receiving five government requests to remove content, three of which were for privacy and security and two for trademark violations.\n11\n1\n“#LibertadNoDisponible: Censura y remoción de contenidos en México [#FreedomUnavailable: Censorship and content removal in Mexico],” Artículo 19, p. 45, accessed April 28, 2021,\nhttps://articulo19.org/libertadnodisponible/\n2\n“Voces contra la indiferencia [Voices against indifference],” Artículo 19, March 28, 2023,\nhttps://articulo19.org/wp-content/uploads/2023/03/Voces-contra-la-Indif…\n.\n3\nEdmundo Morelos, “Yasmín Esquivel Mossa presenta denuncia contra periodistas que exhibieron sus vacaciones en Semana Santa [Yasmín Esquivel Mossa files a complaint against journalists who exhibited her vacations during Easter],” SDP Noticias, April 18, 2023,\n\nhttps://www.sdpnoticias.com/mexico/yasmin-esquivel-mossa-presenta-denun…\n; Diana Lastiri, “La ministra Yasmín Esquivel denuncia a periodistas que publicaron fotos de sus vacaciones en Canadá [Minister Yasmín Esquivel denounces journalists who published photos of her vacation in Canada],” Proceso, April 18, 2023,\nhttps://www.proceso.com.mx/nacional/2023/4/18/la-ministra-yasmin-esquiv…\n.\n4\n5\nAlex González Ormerod, “Powerful Mexicans pay to scrub their reputations online. Lawyers fear it’s hindering criminal investigations,” Rest of the World, February 22, 2022,\nhttps://restofworld.org/2022/mexicans-scrub-reputations-online/\n; Peter Guest, “Exposed documents reveal how the powerful clean up their digital past using a reputation laundering firm” Rest of World, February 3, 2022,\nhttps://restofworld.org/2022/documents-reputation-laundering-firm-elimi…\n6\nPhineas Rueckert, “The Gravediggers: How Eliminalia, a Spanish Reputation Management Firm, Buries the Truth,” Haaretz, February 19, 2023,\nhttps://www.haaretz.com/israel-news/security-aviation/2023-02-19/ty-art…\n.\n7\n“Eliminalia: A Reputation Laundromat for Criminals,” Organized Crime and Corruption Reporting Project, February 17, 2023,\nhttps://www.occrp.org/en/storykillers/eliminalia-a-reputation-laundroma…\n.\n8\n“Eliminalia: A Reputation Laundromat for Criminals,” Organized Crime and Corruption Reporting Project, February 17, 2023,\nhttps://www.occrp.org/en/storykillers/eliminalia-a-reputation-laundroma…\n.\n9\n“Mexico,” Facebook Transparency Report, accessed March 19, 2023,\nhttps://transparency.facebook.com/content-restrictions/country/MX\n\n.\n10\n“Restrictions by country,” Facebook Transparency Report, accessed April 26, 2023,\nhttps://transparency.fb.com/data/content-restrictions/country/\n.\n11\n“Government requests to remove content,” Transparency Report, Google, accessed August 2023,\nhttps://transparencyreport.google.com/government-removals/government-re…\n.\nB3\n1.00-4.00 pts\n0-4 pts\nDo restrictions on the internet and digital content lack transparency, proportionality to the stated aims, or an independent appeals process?\n3.00\n3\n4.00\n4\nDespite some ambiguity in Mexico’s legal and regulatory framework, it and the independent courts offer significant safeguards against arbitrary or opaque restrictions on content, and past attempts to impose new restrictions have faced strong opposition. In 2017, the SCJN declared that blocking an entire website because of alleged copyright violations was unconstitutional, finding it to be a disproportionate measure that infringed on freedom of expression.\n1\nIn November 2022, the First Chamber of the SCJN declared that the so called “right to be forgotten” was incompatible with standards for freedom of expression and access to information, ruling that the obligation to remove personal information about someone who has died from all digital media, established in Mexico City’s civil code, is unconstitutional.\n2\nHowever, the 2020 reform of the Federal Copyright Law regarding intermediary liability negates many of the protections that the courts had previously provided. The reform establishes “notice and takedown” provisions that require online platforms and hosting services to remove any content requested by a copyright holder alleging infringement, without need of judicial authorization or evidence. The intermediary can face fines ranging from 1,000 ($52) to 2,000 ($104) pesos should they fail to comply. The law also empowers those alleging copyright infringement to obtain personal information on the individual accused of posting the offending content, without proof or protective provisions for the alleged offender. Moreover, the reform places the burden on users to prove that content was removed illegitimately, and on platforms to inform takedown requestors about appeals. Additional provisions require internet services to take measures to prevent prior removed content from being uploaded to their respective service a second time, essentially mandating filters. Noncompliance can result in fines. Civil society organizations have criticized the changes for potentially promoting censorship online and endangering users accused of infringement.\n3\nAn April 2023 legislative proposal to create a Federal Cybersecurity Law contains several problematic provisions related to online content regulation.\n4\n\nAmong the provisions, Article 53 requires service providers, social media platforms, and other digital content hosts to comply with orders to \"take down IP addresses, applications, domains, and Internet sites” within 72 hours of receiving notification from the “competent authority,” including a proposed National Cybersecurity Agency. The bill also introduces criminal penalties for broadly defined forms of online expression (see C2). Civil society organizations, such as Article 19 and SocialTIC, have criticized the bill for implementing censorship practices and promoting the militarization of cybersecurity.\n5\nIn recent years legislators have considered proposals to regulate social media platforms that would limit the transparency and proportionality of removals. A bill introduced in February 2021, for example, would empower IFT to oversee social media companies’ content moderation practices, have the final say on content moderation disputes, and overrule companies’ decisions on content removal. IFT would also be empowered to change platforms’ terms of service, impose fines for noncompliance, and establish other rules related to operations and content. The proposed regulations would apply to platforms with over one million users, which would require IFT’s approval to operate in the country. Platforms would also be required to censor speech upon IFT demand, including vaguely defined “hate speech,” “fake news,” and speech that goes against “order and public interest.” Conversely, platforms would be prohibited from censoring content not outlined in the bill. Under the envisioned appeals system, users could escalate appeals on removal decisions with platforms, the telecommunications regulator, and the judicial system.\n6\nCivil society has criticized the bill for risking free expression, imposing regulatory burdens, and fostering indiscriminate censorship.\n7\nGroups have also warned that fines associated with the bill could keep smaller platforms from operating and increase media concentration, and cautioned that it grants IFT arbitrary decision-making power over which platforms can operate and what content they can host.\n8\nIn March 2023, the Tlatelolco Lab, based at the National Autonomous University of Mexico (UNAM), presented the \"Decalogue of Rights of Users of Sociodigital Networks,” a proposal that outlines ten principles to regulate social media in Mexico. Among these points include the “right to a truthful environment on social media.”\n9\nCivil society organizations Access Now and R3D criticized this provision for undermining freedom of expression and taking steps that could establish the state as the arbiter of truth online. Access Now and R3D also criticized the limited role for civil society in the creation of the Decalogue and called for opportunities for civil society to actively participate in ongoing legislative processes to regulate social media platforms.\n10\nNo proposal to regulate social media had passed by the end of the coverage period.\n1\nDiana Lastiri, “Government can’t block web pages by copyright: Court,” El Universal, April 19, 2017,\nhttps://www.eluniversal.com.mx/articulo/nacion/sociedad/2017/04/19/gobi…\n.\n2\n“La primera sala de la SCJN declara incompatible el false ‘derecho al olvido’ con el derecho a la libertad de expresión [The First Chamber of the SCJN declares the false ‘right to be forgotten’ incompatible with the right to freedom of expression],” R3D, November 23, 2022,\nhttps://r3d.mx/2022/11/23/la-primera-sala-de-la-scjn-debe-declarar-la-i…\n.\n3\nCory Doctorow, “Mexico’s New Copyright Law: Copying and Pasting USA’s Flawed Copyright System Is A Human-Rights Catastrophe in The Making,” Electronic Frontier Foundation, July 2020,\nhttps://www.eff.org/files/2020/07/31/mexicos_new_copyright_law.pdf\n; Kit Walsh, “A Legal Deep Dive on Mexico’s Disastrous New Copyright Law,” Electronic Frontier Foundation, July 30, 2020,\n\nhttps://www.eff.org/deeplinks/2020/07/legal-deep-dive-mexicos-disastrou…\n.\n4\nLey Federal de Ciberseguridad [Federal Cybersecurity Law], Gaceta Parlamentaria, Cámara de Diputados, April 25, 2023,\nhttp://gaceta.diputados.gob.mx/PDF/65/2023/abr/20230425-II-2.pdf\n.\n5\n“Cybersecurity bill threatens human rights and promotes militarization [Iniciativa de Ley de Ciberseguridad amenaza los derechos humanos y promueve la militarización ]”, ARTICLE 19, R3D, Social Tic, Cultivando Género and Luchadoras MX, April 27, 2023,\nhttps://articulo19.org/iniciativa-de-ley-de-ciberseguridad-amenaza-los-…\n6\n“Mexico: Online Free Speech at Risk,” Human Rights Watch, April 14, 2021,\nhttps://www.hrw.org/news/2021/04/14/mexico-online-free-speech-risk#\n; Existing law permitted extensive metadata retention and real-time geolocation access subject to judicial safeguards, and a 2023 biometric identity bill raised privacy concerns.", "question_ids": ["1B.9", "4B.4", "4B.9", "7B.6", "7B.7", "8B.10", "8B.2"], "polarity": "mixed", "period_fit": "Directly covers the 2023 reporting period and distinguishes executive-legislative obstruction from later judicial repair.", "ruler_attribution": "The report attributes support for dissolving INAI and the broader institutional posture to López Obrador and his allies; judicial restoration is attributed to the SCJN.", "limitations": ["The metadata law predates López Obrador and includes judicial-warrant requirements for historical data.", "The biometric identity bill was proposed or pending, not established as implemented policy in the extract."], "start_unit": 57, "end_unit": 60, "locator": "extracted HTML block 57 through extracted HTML block 60", "exact_excerpt": "6.00\n6\nArticle 189 of the 2014 Telecommunications Law forces companies to provide users’ geolocation information and other communications metadata to police, military, or intelligence agencies in real time. Article 190 requires providers to maintain records of their users’ metadata for a period of two years, and grants security agencies access to these records at any time.\n1\nIn 2016, the SCJN upheld the constitutionality of the law’s requirements for data retention and real-time geolocation. However, the ruling established the need for a judicial warrant to access historical metadata.\n2\nBeginning in March 2023 and through the end of the coverage period, the INAI, Mexico’s data protection authority and access to information body, was unable to function properly due to the lack of five-person quorum of commissioners. Despite a federal judge ordering the Senate to appoint a commissioner to at least one of the INAI’s three vacant seats,\n3\nthe Senate failed to do so at the end of May, after MORENA legislators accused the judiciary of interfering in its affairs.\n4\nAs of June, nearly 6,000 cases were awaiting resolution by the INAI.\n5\nAmidst the deadlock in May, Human Rights Watch called on the government to appoint commissioners, emphasizing that the INAI’s inability to function left Mexicans vulnerable to potential abuses of their personal data.\n6\nThe Senate’s failure to appoint the INAI commissioners comes amid calls from López Obrador and his allies to dissolve the body entirely and broader efforts by the president to undermine independent regulatory bodies (see A5).\n7\nIn August 2023, after the coverage period, the second chamber of the SCJN ruled that the INAI could meet with only four commissioners until a five-member quorum is appointed, allowing it to begin processing outstanding cases.\n8\nReforms to the criminal procedural code in 2016 required a judicial warrant for government entities to access geolocation data, with some exceptions, such as kidnapping cases in which a person’s life or physical integrity is in danger.\n9\nThe 2016 SCJN ruling also clarified which authorities can access user data; it included federal prosecutors, federal police, and the agency directly in charge of applying and coordinating the National Security Law.\nAn April 2021 reform of the Federal Telecommunications and Broadcasting Law established a biometric cell phone registry that tasked telecommunications companies with collecting biometric data; the SCJN ultimately ruled the registry unconstitutional in April 2022 (see C4).\n10\nThe database would have been accessible to authorities overseeing public security and justice.\n11\n\nDuring the coverage period, Congress again moved to create a biometric database that could undermine the right to privacy. A new bill that the Senate will examine, which was approved by the Chamber of Deputies in March 2023, would empower the Ministry of the Interior (SEGOB) to create and manage a centralized biometric database, the National Registry and Identity System (SID).\n12\nThe SID would require individuals to give biometric information to the SEGOB while registering activities at the Civil Registry, meaning that citizens would effectively have no means to consent to the collection of their personal biometric data.\n13\nCivil society organizations and some opposition lawmakers have raised concerns that the bill does not clearly specify which biometric data would be collected and which entities would have access to it, posing a risk to privacy and the protection of personal data.\n14\n1\nSee Articles governments, and other actors. Mexico remains the most dangerous country in Latin America for journalists and one of the most dangerous in the world.\n1\nArticle 19 documented 696 aggressions against journalists in 2022,\n2\nmaking it the most violent year for journalists since the organization started tracking in 2007. These figures include 181 instances of intimidation and harassment, 151 threats, 48 physical attacks, and 12 murders of journalists in potential relation to their work. Of all reported attacks against the press, 196 (28.19 percent) occurred in the digital sphere, including online intimidation and harassment, digital threats, and attempts to hack journalists' accounts.\n3\nNearly 49 percent of the total attacks against journalists related to coverage of corruption and politics, while 27.59 percent were linked to security and justice issues.\n4\nThe Committee to Protect Journalists (CPJ) separately reported that 13 journalists, including digital reporters,\n5\nwere killed in 2022, making it the deadliest year on record for journalists in Mexico.\n6", "excerpt_sha256": "ab20fc63ab6d9902748506cbd6a00d7515d8a6aa4e5bb3f971c0fed70f429c76", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0031-E001", "source_id": "SRC-2671c8150bc71422", "url": "https://www.iaea.org/sites/default/files/22/09/mexico-gc66.pdf", "title": "México en el Debate General de la 66ª Conferencia General del OIEA", "publisher": "International Atomic Energy Agency", "raw_sha256": "5a55e9c53b714d6365bb41e10bed1f448d69bd1d615b972073aa6c618289ee24", "fact_summary": "México respaldó al OIEA, sus salvaguardias, la no proliferación y el fortalecimiento constante de la seguridad física, nuclear y radiológica; Director General del OIEA,\n\nDistinguidas Delegadas, Distinguidos Delegados.\n\nSeñor Presidente,\n\nEn nombre del Gobierno de México y en representación de la Ing.\nNorma Rocío Nahle García, Secretaria de Energía, deseo felicitarle por\nsu designación como Presidente de la Sexagésima Sexta Reunión\nOrdinaria de la Conferencia General del Organismo Internacional de\nEnergía Atómica.\n\nMéxico desea resaltar la destacada la labor y liderazgo del Director\nRafael Mariano Grossi, al frente del OIEA, para garantizar el\ncumplimiento de los objetivos del Organismo, en medio de\ncircunstancias excepcionalmente difíciles, tras dos años de pandemia\nprovocada por el virus SARS-CoV-2, y sus devastadoras consecuencias\nen los diferentes ámbitos de la sociedad.\n\nAsimismo, mi país elogia la importante labor que realiza el Director\nGrossi, al frente de la misión que se encuentra presente desde el 01 de\nseptiembre de este año, en la central nuclear Zaporizhzhya, en Ucrania;\npor lo que reiteramos nuestro firme apoyo a los esfuerzos del\nOrganismo para garantizar la seguridad de las instalaciones nucleares\nde Ucrania.\n\nMéxico reconoce la labor del OIEA en el cumplimiento de los\ncompromisos internacionales en materia de la promoción de la no\nproliferación nuclear, y el uso pacífico de la ciencia y tecnologías\nnucleares. quiere hacer una especial mención por el 25 aniversario del\nForo Iberoamericano de Organismos Reguladores Radiológicos y\nNucleares, destacando su inestimable labor, encaminada a la\npromoción y Seguridad Nuclear y Salvaguardias, asumió la\npresidencia del FORO, por el periodo de un año, nos sentimos honrados\npor tan alta distinción, al tiempo que elogiamos la publicación del\n“Programa Técnico 1997-2022: 25 años del FORO”, que compila los\nresultados de los proyectos y actividades finalizadas y en desarrollo\ndesde su fundación en 1997.\n\nSeñor Presidente,\n\nEs un gran placer poder comunicarle que, con base al compromiso con\nel ambiente, la seguridad nuclear y la soberanía energética, México\nreconociendo el papel sustancial que la Central Nucleoeléctrica Laguna\nVerde, representa para la generación de electricidad en nuestro país; el\n25 de agosto del presente año, la Secretaria de Energía, Ing. Norma\nRocío Nahle García, autorizó la renovación de la licencia de operación\na la Unidad 2 de la Central Nucleoeléctrica Laguna Verde, por un\nperiodo de 30 años más, a partir del 12 de abril de 2025, al 10 de abril\nde 2055.\n\nEn este sentido, la Central Nucleoeléctrica Laguna Verde, se mantiene\na la vanguardia en la producción de energías limpias del país, como\ngarante del sistema eléctrico nacional, bajo los más altos estándares\ninternacionales de seguridad, en conformidad con el marco regulador\naplicable, garantizando siempre su operación segura y confiable.\n\nSeñor Presidente,\n\nDeseo referirme ahora, al tema del Foro Científico de este año, que lleva\npor nombre “Rayos de Esperanza: Tratamiento Oncológico para\nTodos”.\n\nMéxico reconoce que la iniciativa “Rayos de Esperanza” del Organismo,\nayudará a incrementar las capacidades existentes de los países\n\nMiembros, en la lucha contra el cáncer, y será un apoyo sustancial\ncoadyuvante en el logro de las metas establecidas en la Agenda 2030\nde las Naciones Unidas para el Desarrollo Sostenible, a fin de reducir la\nmortalidad por dicha enfermedad.\n\nSeñor Presidente, Señor Director General, Distinguidos Delegados,\n\nMéxico desea expresar su firme determinación en contribuir al\ncumplimiento del mandato del OIEA, a través de la estrecha y eficaz\ncolaboración que se ha llevado a cabo desde hace más de 64 años, en\npro del bienestar nacional, así como de la comunidad internacional.\n\nPara el gobierno de mi país es prioritario garantizar el ejercicio igualitario\nde los derechos de todas y todos los ciudadanos, sin privilegios, y sin\ndistinciones. En este sentido la cooperación con el OIEA, ha permitido\nque cada vez sean más los mexicanos que se beneficien, a través de la\nutilización de las tecnologías nucleares, en condiciones seguras,\nprincipalmente en los temas de generación de energía eléctrica,\nradioterapia y radiofármacos en la lucha contra el cáncer, así como en\nla mayor calidad de los alimentos, a través de los usos de la irradiación.\n\nMéxico refrenda su papel cómo un actor socialmente responsable,\ncomprometido con la implementación, difusión y búsqueda continua de\naplicaciones pacíficas de la energía nuclear, en pro del bienestar de las\npresentes y futuras generaciones, con el propósito de que nuestra\nsociedad sea más equitativa.\n\n\nMuchas gracias.", "excerpt_sha256": "e08d5cd8358f8d49aac8beb123144b89f21325943bf548bb3b9101eb93bec9ea", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0038-E002", "source_id": "SRC-bd1e2d5a8f2af3bf", "url": "https://www-news.iaea.org/ErfView.aspx?mId=5a43e799-5249-49dd-b649-c5ac897a1dab", "title": "Stolen cat-3 source in Mexico", "publisher": "International Atomic Energy Agency Incident and Emergency Centre", "raw_sha256": "ad7c8cd7b243b344010f371467ee5eb1df7fcdf4ce42cc636fbdd46be2da48a7", "fact_summary": "On 18 October 2023, organized crime stole a radiography camera and vehicle in Ciudad del Carmen, Tabasco; the camera contained a Category 3 Ir-192 radioactive source. Brand: AEA Technology, model: DELTA 880, Serial No. D2810, which houses a radioactive source of Ir-192 with serial number 083H with a current activity of 7.61 Ci.\n\n2. Brand: QSA Global Inc, model DELTA 880, Serial No. D5824, which houses a radioactive source of Ir-192 with serial number 370G with a current activity of 0.11 Ci.\n3. Brand: Source Production & Equipment Co, Model: SPEC-150, Serial No. 2173, which houses a radioactive source of Ir-192 with serial number C0052 with a current activity of 35.64 Ci.\n4. Brand: Industrial Nuclear Co., model IR100, serial No. 4829, which houses a radioactive source of Ir-192 with serial number TT3561 with a current activity of 1.14 Ci.\nThe theft took place on March 22, 2023 at approximately 3:00 a.m. (UTC-6), when armed persons entered the licensee’s facility located in Pánfilo Nájera 205, Col. Lázaro Cardenas, Salamanca, Guanajuato, Mexico. They extracted the four sources and a van.\nOn Tuesday March the 28th, 2023 at approximately 17:30 (UTC-6), The devices were found near to Salamanca Guanajuato due to an anonymous call. The devices were verified by the licensee and all radiological risk was discarded. The devices were recovered and returned to the licensee who took them to its facility. Search for the perpetrators is underway.\nOn Wednesday March the 29th 2023, a group of inspectors of the Regulatory Authority was sent to Salamanca, Guanajuato in order to verify that the devices were safe and the licensee facility had fixed and improved the security systems. At approximately 17:33 (UTC-6), the inspectors had given their approval to the new security measures placed in the facility.\nThe actions taken were: a) The theft has been reported to the law enforcement agencies; b) The National Regulatory body has notified the National Civil Protection Agency; c) Search for the missing industrial radiography devices is underway; d) A search team of the Regulatory Authority has been dispatched to the site in order to help and advice the responders; e) The Regulatory Authority personnel gave theoretical training to local first responders on radiological issues; f) The Licensee filed a legal complaint with the Prosecutor´s Office; g) The devices have been recovered. Radiological risk has been discarded and, e) The regulatory authority has inspected the improved security measures of the facility and found them satisfactory.\nINES Rating:\n1 - Anomaly (Final)\nas per 30 March 2023\nImpact on people and the environment\nRelease beyond authorized limits?\nNo\nOverexposure of a member of the public?\nNo\nOverexposure of a worker?\nNo\nImpact on the radiological barriers and controls at facilities\nContamination spread within the facility?\nNo\nDamage to radiological barriers (incl. fuel damage) within the facility?\nNo\nDegradation of Defence In-Depth\nYes\nOther information\n\nPerson injured physically or casualty?\nNo\nIs there a continuing problem?\nNo\nContact Person for Further Information\nVictor Gonzalez\nComision Nacional de Seguridad Nuclear y Salvaguardias\nvmgonzalez@cnsns.gob.mx\nOther Posts on this Event\n3/22/2023 -\nMissing Dangerous Source at Salamanca, Guanajuato, Mexico.\nSite Sponsors\nThis site is hosted by the International Atomic Energy Agency (IAEA) and co-sponsored by the Nuclear Energy Agency of the Organization of Economic Cooperation Development (OECD/NEA) and World Association of Nuclear Operators (WANO).\nDisclaimer\nThis site is hosted by the IAEA, however, event reports are filed and updated by authorities in participating countries, which remain responsible for all related content.\nRead more >\nRSS Feed\nVersion 7.1.9237, Copyright ©2010-2026 International Atomic Energy Agency (IAEA). Reiteramos\nque éstas no deben usarse por ningún actor, en ningún lugar y bajo ninguna\ncircunstancia.\nSeñor Presidente:\nEn el marco de la Convención sobre Armas Químicas (CAQ), México se\ncongratula por que se ha alcanzado la destrucción completa de todos los\narsenales declarados por los Estados Parte.\nEl pasado 7 de julio de 2023, concluyó el proceso de destrucción irreversible\ndel arsenal químico de Estados Unidos. No se trata de un asunto menor ya\nque supone un paso firme en la dirección correcta en la prohibición y\neliminación de una categoría completa de armas de destrucción en masa,\ny hacia el desarme general y completo.\nNo obstante, cuatro Estados todavía no son Parte de la Convención, por lo\nque México exhorta a dichos países a que se sumen plenamente a ella para\nasí cumplir con la aspiración de su universalización.\nAsimismo, México considera que en esta nueva fase después de la\ndestrucción del arsenal estadounidense, es sumamente relevante definir\nnuevas oportunidades estratégicas para la Organización para la Prohibición\nde las Armas Químicas (OPAQ). Estamos frente a una gran oportunidad,\nespecialmente frente a retos derivados de los avances de la ciencia y\ntecnologías, incluyendo aquellas de uso dual.\nSeñor Presidente:\nSi bien México reconoce los avances alcanzados en la novena conferencia\nde revisión de la Convención sobre Armas Bacteriológicas (CABT), en\nespecial el establecimiento del grupo de trabajo intersesional, todavía no\nestamos cerca del establecimiento de un mecanismo de verificación formal.\n1\nTwo United Nations Plaza, 28th floor, New York, NY 10017\nTel: (212) 752-0220 http://mision.sre.gob.mx/onu/\n\nEn consecuencia, hacemos eco del llamado de la Alta Representante\nNakamitsu para que se tomen pasos decisivos hacia un régimen de\nverificación que dé seguridad sobre el cumplimiento de todas las\ndisposiciones de la Convención.\nEsto es especialmente necesario dado el potencial de nuevas tecnologías\nen materia de biología sintética, biotecnología y biogenética, así como de la\ninvestigación científica, para asegurar el desarrollo de los países y el\ncumplimiento de los Objetivos de Desarrollo Sostenible, particularmente\npor los avances en la salud, la agricultura y la industria, entre otros. No\nobstante, esta misma tecnología tiene un uso dual, lo cual puede dar pie a\nriesgos con un impacto global catastrófico.\nSeñor Presidente:\nFinalizo con una breve referencia al Comité 1540 del Consejo de Seguridad.\nMi país tuvo el privilegio de conducir los trabajos del Comité en 2021 y 2022,\nincluyendo el examen amplio de la resolución 1540 (2004). Algunas de las\nrecomendaciones se centran en la necesidad de mayor atención a los\ncontroles sobre materiales biológicos, donde se registra menos progreso.\nLlamamos a que en esta nueva década de vida del Comité 1540, se avance\ndecididamente en materia de controles biológicos y que dicho foro analice\na profundidad los impactos de las tecnologías emergentes.\nMuchas gracias.\nOOo\n2\nTwo United Nations Plaza, 28th floor, New York, NY 10017\nTel: (212) 752-0220 http://mision.sre.gob.mx/onu/", "excerpt_sha256": "8d52c36666e85a2610fe62d4f7b6a5ca505c8f6c49eccef81c2f65d3a4af2c42", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0044-E001", "source_id": "SRC-99d96e86c8b3085c", "url": "https://treaties.un.org/Pages/ViewDetails.aspx?chapter=26&clang=_en&mtdsg_no=XXVI-9&src=IND", "title": "Treaty on the Prohibition of Nuclear Weapons: status of treaties", "publisher": "United Nations Treaty Collection", "raw_sha256": "41ea53aa9a46801c5d1ac975a2f6a538e54eef373cbae1ec43522d5c2ef2dff0", "fact_summary": "Mexico was a signatory to the Treaty on the Prohibition of Nuclear Weapons from 20 September 2017 and ratified it on 16 January 2018; the treaty was in force during 2023.", "question_ids": ["1B.10", "1B.4"], "polarity": "favorable", "period_fit": "Directly applicable to 2023 treaty status; the underlying decisions predate the period.", "ruler_attribution": "Mexico's treaty position was established during López Obrador's presidency.", "limitations": ["The source establishes treaty status, not implementation, funding, or enforcement measures during 2023."], "start_unit": 5, "end_unit": 12, "locator": "extracted HTML block 5 through extracted HTML block 12", "exact_excerpt": "3.\n4.\n5.\n6.\n7.\n8.\n9.\nSTATUS AS AT : 31-07-2026 09:15:29 EDT\nCHAPTER XXVI\nDISARMAMENT\n9. LA SITUACION EN UCRANIA\nEl Consejo de Seguridad, hay que decirlo, no ha podido cumplir con el mandato que\nle confiere la Carta de las Naciones Unidas, al no haber conseguido prevenir la\nguerra, ni tampoco tomar las medidas que pongan un alto a la agresión armada y\nmenos aún, ha logrado encaminar proceso diplomático alguno para buscar una\nsolución por la vía del diálogo y la negociación.\nDebemos decir que tampoco ha podido lograr que llegue todo el suministro de\nasistencia humanitaria requerido y tampoco, por desgracia, respaldar abiertamente\nla labor del Secretario General y de otros actores, por gestionar el acceso a los\nmercados de granos y fertilizantes producidos por la Federación de Rusia y por\nUcrania.\nAnte esta parálisis, hemos tenido que recurrir a la Asamblea General en una sesión\nespecial de emergencia en la que México, junto con Francia, planteó el marco\nadecuado para canalizar la ayuda humanitaria, a través de la resolución E-11/2, que,\nsin duda, ha sido útil, pero que resulta claramente insuficiente ante la estela de\ndestrucción y sufrimiento que ha dejado la guerra.\nPor tales razones, el Presidente de México, el licenciado López Obrador, consciente\nde la responsabilidad individual y colectiva que tenemos, propone que una comitiva\no caucus de Jefes de Estado y de Gobierno aliente y acompañe los esfuerzos del\nSecretario General, para promover medidas de fomento de la confianza hoy perdida\nentre la Federación de Rusia y Ucrania que permitan generar las condiciones tan\npronto sea posible para acercar a las partes a los mecanismos de solución pacífica de\nlas controversias que señala la Carta de las Naciones Unidas.\nDicho de otro modo, ante la parálisis del Consejo de Seguridad, debemos ofrecer\nentre todos un canal diplomático complementario a los existentes, para interactuar\ncon las partes en conflicto, con miras a reducir las tensiones y encauzar la mediación\nindispensable.\nEsta propuesta ha sido compartida en días pasados con el Secretario General y con\nlas partes involucradas, así como con las delegaciones de la India y de la Santa Sede,\ntoda vez que el Presidente López Obrador considera que en dicha Comitiva debieran\n\nparticipar junto con el Secretario General de la ONU, entre otros, Su Excelencia\nNarendra Modi y Su Santidad el Papa Francisco.\nVamos a seguir con las consultas necesarias, con el único propósito de poder\ncontribuir, como actor imparcial y de buena fe, a generar el más amplio respaldo\nposible a las gestiones y buenos oficios de António Guterres y del Caucus, cuya\nconformación esperamos que proceda, con el apoyo de los Estados Miembros de la\nONU.\nII. PAZ Y SEGURIDAD INTERNACIONALES\nEn estos casi dos años, como miembro electo del Consejo de Seguridad, México ha\nprocurado adentrarse en las causas estructurales de los conflictos y promover la\ndiplomacia preventiva, como elemento central para el mantenimiento de la paz y la\nseguridad internacionales.\nHemos traído a la mesa del Consejo temas que se habían dejado de lado tales como\nla pobreza, la corrupción y desigualdad como factores subyacentes de conflicto, la\nsalud mental como objetivo ineludible de la ayuda humanitaria, las nociones de\nmasculinidades para prevenir la radicalización y el terrorismo, la importancia de\nrespetar el sistema de seguridad colectiva establecido en la Carta, y la necesidad de\nuna coordinación y cooperación para la paz entre los órganos principales de la ONU.\nLos flujos ilícitos de armas pequeñas y ligeras, como resultado de su amplia\ndisponibilidad, vulneran la seguridad regional e internacional. Es por ello que\nMéxico ha insistido en la necesidad de tomar medidas para detener estos flujos de\narmas. Prueba de ello fue lograr que el Consejo de Seguridad adoptara la resolución\n2616 (2021) que está encaminada a respetar los embargos de armas y a que las\noperaciones de paz coadyuven a reforzar las capacidades de las autoridades\nnacionales en el combate al tráfico y al desvío ilícitos de armas.\nHemos pugnado también en favor de la transparencia, la eficacia y la rendición de\ncuentas, y la participación equitativa en los trabajos del Consejo. En ese sentido,\nMéxico va a continuar abogando por una reforma integral del Consejo de Seguridad,\nque incluya más asientos, con términos más largos de mandato, en la categoría de\nmiembros electos, para hacerlo más representativo, transparente y eficiente.\n\nLa parálisis del Consejo de Seguridad es resultado del abuso del mal llamado\nderecho de veto por parte de algunos de sus miembros permanentes. Incluso, durante\nesta Asamblea, dos de ellos se refirieron explícitamente a la necesidad de restringir\nsu uso a situaciones realmente excepcionales.\nEn ese tenor, México y Francia hemos seguido promoviendo que los miembros\npermanentes se abstengan de recurrir al veto ante situaciones de atrocidades masivas.\nInvitamos a los Estados que aún no lo han hecho a sumarse a esta iniciativa que\ncuenta ya con el respaldo de 106 Estados.\nEntre tanto, México y otros 83 Estados, impulsamos un mecanismo de rendición de\ncuentas por el que, cada vez que se registre un veto en el Consejo de Seguridad, la\nAsamblea General se reúna sin dilación para debatir la cuestión e invite al autor o\nautores del veto a explicar su posición. Esta propuesta, que fue adoptada por\nunanimidad mediante la resolución 76/262 y ya se ha activado en dos ocasiones,\napunta en la dirección correcta.\nToca ahora decidir si queremos dar los pasos subsecuentes para evitar que el sistema\nse siga paralizando frente al sufrimiento masivo de personas.\nIII. DESARME NUCLEAR\nLas armas nucleares siguen representando la peor amenaza para la supervivencia\nmisma de la humanidad.\nMéxico lamenta la falta de voluntad política —particularmente por parte de los\nEstados poseedores de armas nucleares— para alcanzar acuerdos que permitan\nlograr dicho propósito, algo que quedó confirmado en la reciente conferencia de\nexamen del Tratado de No Proliferación, que no alcanzó resultado alguno, aún y\ncuando los riesgos de proliferación nuclear son cada día más reales y evidentes.\nMi país coincide con la visión del Secretario General de que un mundo más seguro\ny en paz debe estar asentado en el derecho internacional, la cooperación y la\nsolidaridad y no en la incesante acumulación y modernización de los arsenales\nnucleares y convencionales.\n\nJustamente, fue esta visión la que llevó a los países de América Latina y el Caribe a\nestablecer la primera zona libre de armas nucleares en un área densamente poblada\na través del Tratado de Tlatelolco y la que condujo a la conclusión y entrada en vigor,\nen años recientes, del Tratado sobre la Prohibición de las Armas Nucleares.\nSeñor Presidente, colegas:\nMéxico reafirma que las Naciones Unidas continúan siendo una herramienta única\nde la comunidad internacional, para la resolución pacífica de controversias y la\nconsecución de los objetivos de la Agenda 2030 para el desarrollo sostenible.\nNuestro país seguirá promoviendo el multilateralismo, la solidaridad internacional,\ny la cooperación, como la mejor vía para enfrentar los desafíos globales.\nNo podemos cerrar la puerta al diálogo político y la negociación diplomática. Las\nactuales tensiones internacionales no van a solucionarse por la vía de la fuerza.\nDebemos asegurar entendimientos políticos y mecanismos de construcción de la\nconfianza. Sí, restaurar la confianza es uno de nuestros mayores retos hoy.\nMéxico hace un llamado a todos los Estados Miembros a trabajar conjuntamente\npara alcanzar la paz que todos anhelamos, a trabajar unidos en la recuperación post\npandemia con un enfoque sostenible y resiliente, que contemple cabalmente las\nnecesidades y las prioridades de todos los países, y que ponga a las personas en el\ncentro de nuestras acciones.\nMuchas gracias.", "excerpt_sha256": "466ed7088ef437b492cabc5d321675ace62a4b4220151c51017c8b1059baa40c", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0047-E010", "source_id": "SRC-69497064d8f46c83", "url": "https://elpais.com/mexico/2022-10-01/lopez-obrador-antepone-sus-problemas-de-salud-a-las-fallas-en-politica-de-ciberseguridad.html", "title": "El ciberataque al Ejército revela las fallas en la política de ciberseguridad de México", "publisher": "El País México", "raw_sha256": "bf80f45ac955d98f7ef64bc392e836cfcea92eb76faeca8f738e4534fa9c70d6", "fact_summary": "A 2022 cyberattack exposed more than four million Sedena emails and exposed a serious weakness in the Army's digital security. Experts linked the weakness to austerity-related cancellation of cybersecurity equipment and technology, a general security deficiency, and too few cybersecurity officials and limited institutional capacity. López Obrador acknowledged the hack but, in the reported remarks, did not address the cybersecurity weakness or announce a remedy. The army had also received expanded state responsibilities, including customs and major infrastructure.", "question_ids": ["1B.10", "1B.3", "1B.7", "1B.9", "5B.6", "7B.2", "7B.7", "8B.10", "8B.3", "8B.5", "8B.6", "8B.8", "8B.9"], "polarity": "adverse", "period_fit": "The breach occurred in September 2022; the exposed institutional weakness and reported lack of corrective response are relevant to the 2023 posture.", "ruler_attribution": "The article attributes the expansion of military responsibilities and the president's response to López Obrador; expert explanations of the cybersecurity failure are not proof of his personal intent.", "limitations": ["The article does not establish the complete budgetary or administrative causes of the breach.", "It reports no later 2023 remediation outcome."], "start_unit": 3, "end_unit": 4, "locator": "extracted HTML block 3 through extracted HTML block 4", "exact_excerpt": "Compartir:\nWhatsapp\nFacebook\nTwitter\nBluesky\nLinkedin\nCopiar enlace\nEl\nciberataque sufrido por el sistema de comunicación del Ejército\nha dejado expuestos más de cuatro millones de correos electrónicos, muchos con información confidencial, donde caben desde la reacción de los militares ante el enfrentamiento en 2019 con el poderoso cartel de Sinaloa hasta las múltiples enfermedades del presidente y de miembros de su familia. La mayor filtración de datos del Ejército mexicano, que se recuerda, ha revelado la omnipresencia de las Fuerzas Armadas en la vida nacional y ha dejado expuesta la debilidad del Ejército con una grave falla de seguridad digital.\nEl presidente Andrés Manuel López Obrador ha evitado hablar sobre la falta de ciberseguridad y las consecuencias que estas puedan tener en las próximas semanas. El mandatario ha depositado en el Ejército la responsabilidad de la seguridad nacional y le ha concedido en este sexenio algunas de las más importantes tareas del Estado, como la vigilancia de las aduanas o la construcción de obras civiles de gran calado,\nel Tren Maya o el aeropuerto Felipe Ángeles,\npor citar dos casos. El poder alcanzado por la Secretaría de la Defensa da la medida del impacto de esta filtración confidencial y evidencia la debilidad de las comunicaciones más delicadas del país.\nRápido, los expertos han puesto en cuestión la “frágil” política de ciberseguridad en México, exhibida por el grupo de\nhacktivistas\nGuacamaya, que ya abrió otros preocupantes agujeros en la información que custodian Fuerzas Armadas de países como Chile, Colombia o El Salvador. “Las\nmedidas de austeridad de este Gobierno\nhan cancelado muchas cosas, entre ellas la compra de equipo y tecnología para seguridad informática. El Ejército no tenía la protección debida, lo mismo que ocurrió en Pemex [en noviembre de 2019], que también fue víctima de estas filtraciones. Es una carencia general de seguridad”, afirma el analista de seguridad Guillermo Valdés.\nLas tesis de muchos especialistas insisten en la necesidad de redactar una ley de ciberseguridad. “Con la austeridad sería complicado crear una nueva burocracia, pero quizá [esta tarea] la podría asumir alguna otra instancia, quizá el Centro Nacional de Inteligencia (CNI)”, dice Gerardo Rodríguez, director académico del departamento de Relaciones Internacionales y Ciencias Políticas de la Universidad de las Américas- Puebla. El asunto está presente en las conversaciones diplomáticas: “Uno de los puntos del acuerdo del\nEntendimiento Bicentenario entre México y Estados Unidos\ntrataba sobre aumentar la cooperación en materia de ciberseguridad. Y Antony Blinken, [secretario de Estado de Estados Unidos] insistió en ese asunto recientemente”, asegura Rodríguez. “Por más que México tenga una política de no intervención en otras soberanías, no somos amigos del mundo”, añade el especialista. Y advierte: “Hoy son hacktivistas, pero mañana podrían ser terroristas o potencias extranjeras” las que se cuelen hasta la cocina en los asuntos más estratégicos de México.\nEl Gobierno disolvió al Estado Mayor Presidencial, una institución de origen militar, pero que mantenía su independencia, y ahora está subsumida en la Secretaría de Defensa. “Ese Estado Mayor es quien debería disponer de las informaciones relacionadas con la vida del presidente y de su familia que han salido a la luz, pero el presidente lo desapareció y lo fundió con el Ejército”, dice Valdés. La medida se revela ahora en todo su riesgo, al parecer de este especialista, que fue jefe del servicio de inteligencia mexicano entre 2007 y 2011. “Esa información podría ser usada para chantajear al presidente, por ejemplo. Y si los militares tuvieran tentaciones golpistas, mejor sería que no dispusieran de esos datos. El Estado Mayor debería ser una rama separada y fiel a la Presidencia”, sugiere.\nValdés cree que el Ejército dispone hoy de más información de la que cabría suponer, debido, asegura, a la estrecha relación que ha forjado el presidente y su familia con los militares y la cantidad de tareas cruciales que les han asignado. “Las revelaciones de hoy confirman hasta qué punto el presidente ha permitido al Ejército estar cerca de él y de las decisiones de poder”.\nLa colosal filtración de correos electrónicos llega en un momento político que la desnuda de inocencia. Valdés no cree que sea casualidad el tiempo elegido para hacer públicas estas comunicaciones, es decir, para evidenciar la debilidad el sistema de ciberseguridad mexicano. “Justo ahora se está discutiendo\nla fuerte presencia y el papel que juega el Ejército en la vida pública\n\nde país. Parece claro que se quiere mostrar la debilidad de la Institución militar. Es como decir: ‘cuidado con el Ejército y toda la información que posee”. Mucha, para tanta vulnerabilidad.\nValdés lamenta que este Gobierno haya “degradado al CNI al situarlo bajo el mando de la Secretaría de Seguridad Pública, como si la seguridad nacional fuera un tema menor”. Explica que alrededor de 2008, la Inteligencia mexicana, el entonces CISEN, “coordinó un proyecto de ciberseguridad donde participaba todo el gabinete de seguridad, pero el proyecto quedó cancelado hace años. El asunto se estancó”, asegura. “Al presidente no le ha importado la ciberseguridad”, critica. “Es un tema central que debería atender el CNI de forma seria”.\nEn buena medida, coincide con estas opiniones Guillermo Rodríguez, aunque cree que “la supuesta falta de interés del presidente no quiere decir que no exista en otras instituciones del Estado, como Defensa, Marina, el CNI o la Inteligencia Científica. El problema es que son pocos los funcionarios dedicados a la ciberseguridad, que rozan el centenar en la Agencia y deberían ser unos 300. México tiene capacidad para invertir en esta área. De lo contrario, seguiremos perdiendo importancia como potencia media”, señala Rodríguez. Explica que la Guardia Nacional heredó una estructura profesional en esta materia, pero pequeña, que debería crecer. “No podemos dejar de lado estas amenazas”.\nRodríguez pone de ejemplo lo ocurrido en Estonia, que sufrió su primer ciberataque, “patrocinado por Moscú”, hace más de 15 años y tomó la decisión de crear una infraestructura fuerte de ciberseguridad y ciberdefensa. “Hoy está en los niveles más altos en esa materia”.\nMéxico ha vivido este jueves esa desagradable experiencia, que puede airear los problemas más íntimos de la familia presidencial, algunos sin interés ciudadano alguno, quizá ni estratégico, solo alimento para el morbo. Interesa desde luego la salud de presidente y López Obrador no ha tenido inconveniente en reconocer, desde punto y hora, que está “enfermo”. Tampoco era la gran revelación, habida cuenta de que se conocen sus problemas coronarios, que ya le causaron un infarto en 2013 y\nque le someten a cuidados especiales continuos.\nNada ha mencionado de la debilidad del sistema de ciberseguridad mexicano ni de posibles intenciones de solventarlo.\nSuscríbase aquí\na la\nnewsletter\nde EL PAÍS México y reciba todas las claves informativas de la actualidad de este país\nSobre la firma\nCarmen Morán Breña\nVer biografía\nTrabaja en EL PAÍS desde 1997, donde ha sido jefa de sección en Sociedad, Nacional y Cultura. Ha tratado temas de educación, asuntos sociales e igualdad.
